PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

(By Redcap)

One of my readers some distance away, compltic% of great trouble amongst his stock from rats. These beastly rodents are most difficult vermin to deal sith. They not only destiny stock, but play brace by gnawing walk and floors and at time, they even block drains. Often enough they do more harm by eating corn and meal, thin by any de,4ruction to the poultry themselves. A good and well traiLed terrier slog is undoubtedly an asset to any poultry yard, but when the rodents are existent in large numbers, even a dog cannot keep them down.

should always be rigidly avoided, beauce however careful you are in placing it. sooner or later a valu- able bird or a favGurite dog may become the victim of your best intentions. Some people recommend asphyxiating with bisulphide of carbon. If the rats are located in a detached house or if they inhabit one special place the following plan i 4 a good one : Block op all tl.e boles but one, then pour into the latter sonic of the chemical named above, and block that one also. The vapour from the bisulphide of carbon will search every hole and corr.er, asphyxiat- ing every rat that it reaches. Care should be used 1 when adopting this method not to use a lamp, as the chemical is explo.ive. A friend of mine found the following method also a good one :

PLASTER OF

Place acme fiats oatmeal on a plate where the rata mostly congregate. If neat morning you find the meal has been eaten, give rather more the follow- ing night. The rata, having found a feast, will bring their friends along with them ultimately, then you can proceed with your plan of destruction. After a few nights feeding, proceed by mixing an equal bulk of extra dry plaster of Paris with the meal. It most however be well mixed. To avoid detection add to the mixture a little white thirds. Close to the meal llace a flat dish of water. The plaster will cause inflammation arid a violent thirst. The rats will drink the water only to seal their doom, frr most surely this will be their last meal. The plaster will " act" in their stomachs like granite and, they will never recover. Ile sure that the previous night you have so secured your fowls, that they cannot get out to eat any that nay be left.

The ordinary method of catching rats with cage traps is rather a slim, one. Try the following contrivance: —Provide an iron corn bin. about 2 feet. deep, with 80M0 bait at the bottom ...twit as meal with a few pieces of cheese naked with it ; The latter being of a strong 810.11, entices them more quickly. Place a board to the edge of the cora bin for them to get up, the rats will thim jump down to the bait, but to get out it is impossible. Care should be taken before adopting this method to remove every trace of food stuffs, her,use you will not be able to persuade them into dangerous places, if they can get their fill elsewhere. lly all means and stall times where rats congregate, have a few cage trans set ; bat if you are pestered with them rodents in a large degree, then try any of the aforequentioaed plans, 80 that you may effect a clearing out.

In hunting di.tr:cts it is a difficult matter indeed to keep poultry. Many a farmer has given up in disgust —especially those who have gone in for prize poultry. The compensation given by the hunting fraternity is nearly always inadequate, end even if it were so, it is eery ann,ying to lose a few good show birds. The poultry tinnier finds as much op: rt in breeding a " Palace winner "as does the m.,n in the red coat when riding a fox to death. And tarn sure the latter pastime is more cruel. Therefore I think that the poultry fetich, rught to receive a little more eon- sideratir n than there people are wont to give. Where foxes abound it is a difficult snorter t protect the fowls and I .Irould always advise you to farce in any valuable st ci; always placing a strand of burl ed wire along the top of the fence. A well-trained dog is about nag, cd a protection as I know of. It is not often that Reynard will approach the poultry pens if he only hears tie bark of a dog. He is far too cunning a " gentleman' t v run any risks. As a further proof of the fox nuisance I include the follow- ing letter which was sent to Poultry "in March Let.

"I know readers of " Pori-MT " will be sorry to hear of irry wife's misfortune. Yesterday (March 21111) at mid-day a fox came into one of our orchards and killed a trio of silver Sebrights she was breeding from. All the birds were bred and reared at Fox Hill, aml only a few weeks ago my wife refused £l5 for them from an American customer. The cock was 2nd Tamworrh, 2nd Newark, and 3rd Penistone ; while the ' two prillr ts had won fat 'bywords Heath, 1910, Ist • and silver challenge cup, Newark-on-Trent, IMO. and many other prices. The fox queetion is becoming a very sericrus matter both to fancy and utility breeders of poultry." 'I he shove letter was sent to the editor of' Poultry' by Mr. R. Fletcher Hearshaw, of Burton Joyce, and with him I agree that it is a nerious question. In many caner, a man's livelihood depends up-rn his poultry, bad yet Le has to labour without adepate protection. I hope this may catch the eye of some fra.hunier with the hope that something tangible will be done for the poultry farmers in general.

EM 111. EGOS.

...... The general canoe of small eggs in through the bird being too fat, whbh in nearly every cite is due to wa.t of exercise or feeding on too fattening foods. When you have a peicentage of very small eggs yGu should find ant the cause so that you may effect a remedy. If ibriugh being too fat, you can generally tell by I [milling the boils, and if on doing so you find they are plump nod heasy, you should - without delay—reduce the feeding and add Epsoen Salt, to the drinking water. Alai provide them with mere exercise. If they have not a free range, you should bury the grain in about four inches of litter no that they have to scratch for their foot/. If !he shell if the eggs is thin, a small piece of unslaked lime added to the drinking water will prove beneficial, be careful in doing that you use on'y a very small piece.

(.11EFS-110NF. ND Yu( NO CHF 11,. I have hen avked if green-bane is a good thing for

young chicks ? Green-bone is a very good thing for chickens if given judiciously. At first it she uld be given very sparingly ant in no ca... ',mild it be given until the chubs are at least four or five weeks old, then yon may give them a feed f the bone about three times per week. In hot weather green-bone is preferable to meat 'wean-a it keeps sweet much longer ; not only so, lan his considerably cheaper.

111'Ng.

How easy it is to forget ! I have been askcd to name a few of the bebt t.rends for a confined run. I have often gone into the above question in previous articles. Neverthele.s I am only too pleased to be of any aseistance and it s ould help us all if readers lx th ten and o!el would only ask more questions. The best varieties for egg production al ne inn small and confined area are Leghorn. (any variety) Minorcas ; (if the run is not too exposed) Andalusian. and At cocas. Yon will note that I have not mentioned any of the heavier breeds. There are good r, aeons for not doing .ci, because the general tendency with these is over- fatness, Of course in town runs where there is only a back-yard for rooting quarters, but where alto they have access to open spaces, then the heavier varieties may be kept prolitab:v. In the latter case a good strain of almost any of the Asiatic breeds may be kept. But alter all, if for egg-production alone, there is none to heat the lloudan first cross with elmoet env of the Mediterranean car ietie, I have bad splendid results from the lloudan White Leghorn cross. They are vet itable laying machines.

THE COLLEGE, CHESTER

Whitsuntide Educational Conference

The Princirrl and Committee of Chester Training College, are embarking this Whitsuntide on an enter- prise of unique and most interesting character. While the present students are enjoying their vacation, their places in the college are to he occupied by old students, who will assemble for an Educational Conference, lightene I by sari°tn functions of a social and recrea- tive character. This re-union which is probably the first of its kind, will last front Friday, 'Sind June, till Whit Monday, during which time the old students will have an opportunity of living over again the life of their strident days. On Friday night when the ex.studenta assemble, there will ten social gathering. Srturday will be devoted, in the morning to a conference on the Curriculum of the Prinrsry School," opened with a paper by Mr. Reynolds, of the School of Tech- nology, Mrtnchester, and in the afternoon to excursion, dos. On Sunday there will be a service in the beautiful College Chapel, alway. the Mecca of old Chester men, and the attractions of the Cathedral awl on Monday, the final day, the usual annual reunion ot old students, with the accompanying festivities, will probably attract a large addition to the numbers already prtirent. The undertaking is a large one, and most Involve much self-denying labour on the part of the Principal It ov. It. A. Thomas) and staff. We hope and believe it will lien huge success, and congratulate the College on its enterprise.

At. meeting of the Finance Committee on May 19th, the Clerk reported that on the examination of the certificates which Mr. George Holford, vaccination officer of the Warrington district, had forwarded along with hie account, it was found that a number of forms other than certificates of successful primary vac:ina. Lions had been submitted and charged for by him as certificates of tuccessful primary vaccinations. (Jo investigation it weft found that Mr. llolford had pre. vion-ly charged in his accounts for forms other than certificates of succeasful primary vaccinations, and for which he wart not entitled to make any charge whatever. The Clerk summoned Mr. Holford to attend at his office at 10 30 that morning. Mr. linlford at once edmitted that he had knowingly dt no wrong in charging for forms other than certificates of successful printery v..ccioa- tone, and stated that his reason for so doing sae to make up the loni in fees he had unstained by moon of a number of persons having taken advantage of the exemption clause in the N'accination Act. The Clerk stated that Mr. Lowry, Local Govern- ment Board In-pector, hail called upon hint, and on the matter being reported to him he stated that in ht opinion the Local Government Board would uphold the Guardia.' action if they suspended Mr. Holford from duty. The committee recommended that Mr. Holford he suspended from the office of vaccination officer, which he holds in this Colon, that the Clerk be instructed to report the matter to the Local Government Board anti also that the Clerk be authorised to arrange for the carrying out of the duties in the meantime. . . .

On the motion of Mr. Joi.bex iseconded by Mr. Girt, the minutes were approved. The CLERK elated that be immediately reported the matter to the Local Government Board and had received a reply on folloan:—'' In reply I am to state that if the Guardiana decide to remove Mr. Ilolford from his °ince an vaccination officer, under article 5 r f the Vaccination Order, 167 I, the Board should he furniebed with a copy of the resolution of the Guardians setting Oct the charges against Mr. llolford and asking for the Board's consent to his removal from office." Mr. lit tuf sof: moved that the matter be referred to the Finance Committee for full investigation. Ile said the probability was that if certain routine duties had been properly athered to Mr. lloltord, eh° bad twenty-seven years' servioe to bin credit, would not been been found in such an undesirable nosition. . . .

ST. hELENS VICAR'S NEW POST.

The Rev. T. W. ketchlee, who has been for nearly eight years vicar of St. klark's Church. St. Helens, and 'rho was previously vice-principal of the Bishop of Liverpool's hostel, hae been appointed vicar of Si. , Saviour's Battersea Park, London. Mr. Ketcblee is an M.A., of Exeter College, Oxford. Before going to Liverpool he was curate at Eastbourne and All Saints', South Lambeth. At St. Helens he Lae accomplished large 'mount of good work ; a new children's hall has been built at • Cost of £5,000, and a great deal has been done to beautify the interior of the church, the chancel of which is now undergoing a complete trans- formation. Mr. Ketchlee will leave St. Heleus in September. He ie a native of London.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

"SETS FREE."

The canstant recurrence of this ann:uncement in the advertisement columns of the paper irresistibly recalls a little witticism in which a certain tailor was wont to indulge. Here it is " These trousers only is. (W. per leg; seats free." That may serve by way of " kick-off "; now to be serious. WORKERS' EDUCATIONAL ASSOCIATION.

Warrington is likely to have its Univernity I Tutorial class, and it is possible that may lead to the formation of a local branch of the Worket.' Educe- tioxal Association. The arrangement strikes me as odd, but that must he because at other csntres of which I happen to have experience, the order has been reversed first has crane the W.E A., comoosed of members of the Trades and Labour Councils, Co-operative Societies, Sunday schools, Mutual Improvement Son, ties, and a° like, and the Tutorial class has with Collll:irative case been funnel there- from. However, process. s and modes if procedure are of small account; " the p'ay's the thing." The objects and methods cf the Association jut now in 'question were recently set forth in an appeal rimmed by Pr, Tensor Sadler, whose acquaintance I hail the good fortune to make while yet he Inns an ordinary Cniversity extension l-cturer, the bead master of flepton (Mr. William Temple), the Bishop of Birmingham, the Dean of Durham mid several Labour leaders With great interest I rend how in the busiest industrial certres this organisttion of workingmen is gathering great classes of men awl women students after the day's work is done, not for an intellectual dissipation in the way of popular lectures, but for steady, continued, systematic study. ()cer 2,0,0 workers were even thus early engaged in the three years' course (ride last week's explanatory notice) not in order that they may command a higher salary or seek to rai-e themselves out of their own clams, but simply that their minds may Ire trained to use and at preciate books. Truly, as was remarked at the time, this hanger for knowledge is the best and putest of all human positions; its exi-ttence is the hope or the future. But, as I understand, the time for the inaugnration of the more comprehensive movement in Warrington is not yet. Meanwhile, the Tutorial Class project has the warmest and whole-hearted advocacy of the present writer among others who state wla(t they do knots and testify what they have seen of the benefits and blessings of this disinterested and splendidly mewled agency. Many of us Will be much disappointed arid not a little ashamed of our district if the workers do not make the best of the unique opportunity ZIOW prf seated; hut I fancy I know them letter than to suppose theta capable of anything to foolish. " TIIE WHACKING OF THE SMALL BOY,"

Thus begin another" Examiner "note of this day week, and I In ry supplmnent it here. It seems only a few weeks ago since! read a anewhere that Dr. W. 11. D. Rouse, headmaster of the Pee. Grammar School, was asked w healer he coreddered that ha had the , right to cane • boy who had sta3ed away from school when told to do so by hi, father. The answer (as they say in ParliamsnO was in the aflitmative. Simul- taneously it te Ann ired that at Eton the Rev. Edward Lyttleton had in the Upper School substituted the cane for the birch. There nhe evidt ntly differa born the II ighmaster of the Msnchester Grammar School, who a few Tears ago gave up the cane and went bark to the birch. AV bat I want to emphasize is that only boys attending the leading public schools must now-a- days suffer the in lignity of corporal punishment. The duke's son—the "con of a belted earl may be whacked with impunity ; but let a relons:ble and much enouring teacher in me of our elementary schools administer a little wholesome correction to Dick, Tom, or Harry, and he will be in danger of the judgment—he will indeed be lucky if he escape merely with an interview with an irate and possibly violent though seriously mistaken parent, and may , have to answer a summons before the local magis- crates, I observe that a contemporary old enough no -I-now better wonders "horn much longer the teachers of our youth will tolerate a system f education which , can only he kept going with the aid of a penal ayatem so abominable and degrading " ma that involving the use of cane or birch, My own sympathies are strongly and decidedly— but I will illustrate my point by means of an incident I came across the other day. "Thin book," remarked the bouse-to-hoose merchant, aa he dexterously insinua.ed one f of in the doorway, and smiled pleasantly, " is well-narned 'Tine Mother's (table.' With its aid you ran bring lip your children pr. petty." Ilia victim thoroughly examined the binding, and felt the weight of the book Then she gripped it by the edge with her right band, and brought it down—whack !—on the other. To the a..tonistied salesman, she appeared to be trying to see how convenient it was to handle : lint for what pur- pose ? " I don't thank I'll take nor," she remarked at last. " I'm sure it's no better than the orditary slipper !" WHAT TO DO IN A THUNDERSTORM._

....,.. . Last week's reports of the great etorm and damage by lightning which recurred in these rarts brought to my mind a terse summary in " Chamber's Journal " of stable advice given in a new edition of his " Modern Lightning-conductors" In Mr. Killingworth Hedges, M. Inst. C.E. Some of the suggestions are familiar or obvious, a few may almost be said to be impracticable or at least a counsel of perfection. However, I may give the gist of them for what the leader may de( m them vr..rth. Shelter- ing under trees should be avoided, as well as standing in the vicinity of spreading branches ; nor should one! atand on the lee-side of a haystack or building, as the! rain dripping from the top thereof may easily lead the flash in the direction of one's person, c inverting one into a human lightning-con. doctor. Telegraph posts should be given a wide berth, and the telephone should not be used during a storm. Lightnieg-conductors, rain- , water pipes, and such like should be avoided. If one is walking over open country and becoming saturated with the rain, the best safeguard is to crawl under a low copse or hedge. Should such be unavailable, as on a plain or moor, the safest pro- cedure is to lie that down. To hold up an umbrella as shelter against the rain is to seek disaster. Similarly, should one be cycling when overtaken, one should dismount and leave the cycle on the ground. It is not that the cycle directly attracts the lightning, as is generally supposed, but a flash throws out innumer- able ',ranch (discharges. If one's clothes are dry one may receive no further injury than a smart shock, but if the garments are sodden with the rain fatality may easily result. The safest place in within the house. True, the building may be struck, but the chalices are very remote. If such should result, it is invariably the chimney-stack which is selec'ed by the flash in its passage to earth, and consequently one should stan I well clear of the fireplace, which invariably will be blown into the room. Hot-water and other pipes uld likewise be avoided. The windows should be shut, for glass is an excellent insulator, and will not attract lightning as is popularly believed. One should not nit in a conservatory or greenhouse, for the trail- ing wires and general metallic work may be highly charged with electricity resulting from a flash ; and one ehonld not shelter in a farm building where animals are collected.

THE EXAMINER.

SAITRDAY, MAY 27 1911.

However exasperating LORD the LORD CHANCELLOR LOREBURN'S may seem to be in con-

DECLARATION. nection with the

appointment of magis- trates, he is without doubt a great Liberal in all other respects. No previous occupant of the Woolsack has addressed to the Peers such advanced and doughty speeches as those of LORD LOREBURN. They are speeches, moderate in tone, but most resolute in opinion. They are always listened to with respect, as we know ; but amongst those to whom they are addressed there must also be a sort of wonder that such things can be said in the House of Lords, and especially by a LORD CHANCELLOR whose manner is so quiet, so dignified, and so persuasive. The ARCHBISHOP OF CANTER- BURY said on Wednesday that there has of late been a great change with regard to the situation of the two Houses. It is a change, he added, which has been produced in the main, no doubt, by the reverberations occasioned by the rejection of Mr. LLOYD GEORGE'S second Budget. Fear, in Collins's od3 to the Passions, was" startled by the sound himself had made." The House of Lords has been similarly affected. But so far as there has been any leaning towards more reasonable views and a more temperate behaviour, it ought in large measure to be attributed to the influence of LORD LOREBURN. On Wednesday, the LORD CHANCELLOR made a statement which is likely, for party purposes, to be widely misrepresented. It will be said, in all probability, that the oxupant of the Woolsack is more moderate than his colleagues of the Cabinet. It may

even be asserted that he has thrown over the members of the Government sitting in the other House. This will be on the grounds that he has declared the readiness of the authors of the Bill to consider such amendments as the Lords may be dis- posed to make to the Parliament Bill. To those who have followed the proceedings on this measure throughout, it will be unneces- sary to say that LORD LOREBURN merely repeats in this instance what has been said again and agair by other important members of the Government. The idea that amend- ments were not to be considered is an inven- tion of the enemy, of course. Amendments proposed by the Opposition have already been considered on a rather extensive scale. How otherwise could the Bill have taken up so much time in the House of Commons What the Government is opposed to is not amendment of the Parliament Bill where that is possible, but any abandonment of its principle. If the measure is passed through the House of Lords in a form which bars the Peers from interference with finance, and which makes an end of the absolute veto in regard to other measures, there will, we imagine, be no difficulty about the acceptance of amendments of form.

Lord CROMER is beyond question one of the most able men in the House of Lords, yet, in opening the debate in that Chamber on Wednesday, he made a speech that now seems curiously belated. He argued at length in favour of control over finance, though this, as we have pointed out again and again, has been abandoned by Lord LANSDOWNE, who made his surrender all the more conspicuous and notable by asserting the right of the Peers to interfere with money Bills whilst declaring that they no longer proposed to exercise it. Lord CROMER boldly puts for- ward the contention that control over finance by the House of Lords is necessary in the interests of the people. Sound finance, he says, accurately enough, is the basis of all good government. From this safe proposition he advanced into ambiguity, speaking of the reckless manner in which liabilities of known amount had been incurred without the least idea of how they would be met. This is not merely vague, but, as we suspect, inaccurate. LORD CROMER'S point of view may be discussed from his condemnation of what he calls the unnecessary sacrifice of the sugar duties. If that is bad finance, we shall undoubtedly see some more of it. But LORD CROMER will not recommend the House of Lords to public favour by presuming that if it were left with its present powers it would stand in the way of a free breakfast table. Neitber will he help their lordships much by the suggastion that under other conditions than those now prevailing they would have opposed Old Age Pensions and the National Insurance Bill.

The census returns for CENSUS FACTS Great Britain will soon AND FIGURES. be complete, and we shall know exactly what the population of these islands is, and a good deal of personal detail about the people comprised in the total. The figures for England and Wales show an increase of three-and-a-half millions—the Tnallest per- centage-10'91 per cent.—on record. There are many interesting comparisons arising out of the details. London proper contains 4,522,961 people, a decrease of 13,306 in the ten years; the outer London shows a large increase, making the grand total for the Metropolis of 7,252,963—the largest congregation of human beings in a small area that the world has ever known. Liverpool beats Manchester with a total of 746,567, against 714,427, but add Salford's 231,380, and greater Manchester is only a couple of thousand behind Liverpool, Bootle and Birkenhead combined War- rington's new total of 72,178 compares very favourably with 64,242 in 1901, and represents a ratio of increase of 1225 per cent., which is quite up to the average of the County Boroughs. Rather curiously there are 519 more males than females in Warrington, so that the old saying of " three women for every man" does not hold good here. The ladies ought to feel the benefit of the keen competition due to a considerable masculine surplus. St. John's is still our most populous ward, with 10,623 inhabitants, but Latchford and Whitecross run it very close with 10,020 and 10,001 respectively.

It is encouraging to SCHOOL HYGIENE. find that the Education

Committee is taking prompt steps on the important question of the health conditions in the elementary schools of the borough. The annual report of the Medical Officer (Dr. J. Coate Hibbert) disclosed a state of affairs which demands serious consideration and for this purpose a special meeting is to be held on Monday evening next. The Medical Inspec- tion Committee has already discussed the report and submits a series of helpful comments on the more striking features. With regard to the schools in which defects in sanitation and ventilation have been noted, the committee has come to the conclusion that there are six cases (Latchford R.C., Sacred Heart, St. Alban's, St. Ann's, St. Peter's and Trinity Mixed) urgently demanding attention, and it is proposed to require the managers to take steps to deal with the conditions without further delay. Another section of the report laid emphasis on the large number of chil- dren who suffer from bad teeth, and we are glad to see that the Education Committee is invited to consider the best means of making provition, subject to the sanction of the Board of Education, for the treatment of , dental defects. The question of school hygiene 1 is fraught with immense importance to the future of the community and it is to be hoped that as the outcome of the special meeting practical steps will immediately be taken to ensure that, as far as it lies in the power of the authorities, the children shall not be handicapped in the race of life by drawbacks which might be prevented or remedied.

The judgment in the THE POWER OF East Nottingham THE PURSE, election petition reveals the startling fact that, in the present state of things, the law is absolutely ineffective against the advantage which the rich man possesses in a political conteet. The judges hold that charity dis- tributed through a political agent, from party offices, and confined to one political division of a town in which distress was general, does not invalidate an election, but must be regarded from the legal standpoint as charity and nothing else. If this is the

law, the conditions between a rich candidate and a poor candidate are absolutely unequal, and unless it is drastically altered, the inten- tion of Parliament in establishing Payment of Members to enable the State to utilise the zeal and ability of poor men in the work of legislation will be nullified.

Mr. Balfour has been THE BANKRUPTCY taking the meeting of OF IMPERIAL the Imperial Confer- PREFERENCE. ence as an opportunity to revive the old bogey of disruption of the Empire as the conse- quence of this country's adherence to the principles of Free Trade. The object-lesson eof the Imperial Conference is all the other way. The bonds of sympathy and friendship between the Mother Country and her Colonies have in recent years been drawn very much closer, and the Imperial Conference is dis- cussing various ways of co-operation which will redound to the mutual advantage of the units of the Empire without impairing the sense of perfect freedom in each to pursue the line of conduct which is prescribed by local conditions. Thelparty which persists in limiting its conception of co-operation bet wean the units of the Empire to a mere trade bargain is loudly proclaiming itself bankrupt in Imperial ideas and ideals.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Mr. F. E. Smith says that " if Liverpool and Manchester think alike, it does not matter a brass farthing what the rest of England thinks." Then let us split the differ- ence (says the "Morning Leader") and give a controlling vote to Warrington. • • The 25th anniversary of Mr. Asquith's election as M.P. is to be celebrated on July 22nd at East Fife by a presentation to the Premier, his wife and daughters, from his constituents. Already there are over 2,600 subscribers, and the project is being carried forward with the utmost enthusiasm.

The whole scheme Of Protection is for the plunder and oppression of the common people of all lands. It is one of the sub- stitutes for chattel slavery. It accomplishes the same results—the robbery of labour— but in a more refined and elegant way.— From "The Tariff," by L. F. Lybarger, Philadelphia.

• • • The debate on the National Insurance B.

in the Commons on Thursday, was marked by a striking tribute to the success of the working of the Labour Exchanges. Mr. Churchill (the Home Secretary) said that the Exchanges created a " record' last week, when 12,800 persons were placed in situations exclusive of casual jobs. It is expected that 800,000 situations will be filled in the current year.

• • The cotton trade in Germany isnot all beer and skittles at present. A Lancashire manufacturer has this week received a letter from one of the highest authorities on the cotton trade in Germany, which says "Trade in Germany is beastly rotten. The fine spinners have orders booked, though at I bad prices; but course counts are lying in the streets. Most of the manufacturers are worse off than the spinners."

The late Dr. E. M. Grace was a pap Aar idol at his native village of Thornbury, and none the less because he would never be , "out" when playing with the Thornbury Club. If clean bowled he would coolly knock the stumps in again, and say "Not at Thornbury." This puts one in mind of Sir Arthur Priestly, M.P., when he went to I America with Ranjitsinhji's team. In one match a ball fairly took "Skipper" Priestley's middle stump, but nonchalantly remarking " I never could play those trial balls,' he calmly continued batting. • • -

Mr. and Mrs. A. H. erosfield entertained a party at dinner on Thursday night in honour of Mr. Schlytter, the President of the International Association for Labour Legislation. The party included Sir Thomas Oliver (president of the British section of the association), the President of the Board of Trade and Mrs. Buxton, Lord and Lady Shaw, Lord and Lady Swaythling, Baron and Baroness Berlepach, Sir Alfred Mond, Mr. and Mrs. Winkworth, Lady Acton and Mr. and Mrs. Emmott. Sir Thomas Oliver and Mr. Crosfield are the British representa- tives on the Council of the Association.

In these days there is something of piquancy in the verses which Macaulay dashed off when a former Reform Bill was hanging in the balance : What though now opposed I be, Twenty peen shall c trry me; 11 twenty won't, thirty will, For I'm hie. Malesty'a lymncing Bill. . . .

In graver mood he 'rte of theunight when the Bill passed the Commons by a majority of one. 'I called a cabriolet, and the first thing the driver asked was, 'ls the Bill carried?' Yes, by one.' 'Thank God for it, sir." "So ended," he goes on, "a scene which will probably never be equalled till the reformed Parliament wants reforming, and that I hope willi not be till the !days of our grandchildren." So it has come to pass. 4.

The owners of a well-known newspaper circulating in Mr. Lloyd Georges constitu- ency are getting ready a souvenir of th., forthcoming Royal visits to North Wales. The contents will include portraits of the King, the Queen, the Prince of Wales, and Mr. Lloyd George. Advertisements are being canvassed for, and the following terms are, offered to advertisers who wish their announcements to appear on the pages opposite the various portraits : Facing King George l2 guineas. Facing Queen Mary Facing the Prince of Wales 1(1 guineas. Facing Mr. Lloyd George l5 guineas. This advertisement is made all the more interesting by the fact that it is issued by a Tory firm. _ -

The delegates to the railway clerks' wit- I ference at Liverpool on Saturday were, like other visitors, struck with the wretched condition of many of the street children of the city. During the trip of t'ae Flying Kestrel on the Mersey, Mr. J. A. Seddon, the late M.P. for the Newton Division, collected 255. from the delegates in order to give the youngsters a treat in celebration of the conference. When the boat returned to Liverpool an adjournment was made to Goree-piazzas, where a number of ragged children were invited to enter a neighbouring cocoa-rooms to partake of a good meal. At first the children were sceptical, but once it was realised that the news was true the place was invaded by a swarm of gamins of both sexes. A big policeman bore down on the premises in the interests of order, and when he found out the cause he offered sixpence towards the cost of the entertain- ment. A workman standing by added another shilling. Altogether one hundred and seventy-two children received a good meal, consisting of a pint of tea, two pieces of bread and butter, and a slice of combeef. They were.waited upon by delegates superintended by the ex-M.P.

A report just issued by the New York Charity Organisation Society on living con- ditions in that city, states that there are now over 100,000 beggars in the city of New ork. This number is a record, but the society reports that the total is increasing to an alarming extent. The Charity Organisa- tion Society has received more than a third more requests for help during the four months of this year than it did in the corres- ponding period of 1910. The report states that the majority of the cases are really necessitous, and are not professional beggars or "won't works." The distress is particularly acute in the East Side tenement district. The society attributes the increase of poverty to bad trade and quietness in the labour market. Industrial disputes and the , numerous recent strikes have had their' effect, but just now, despite the Psotectionist fiction that there is always work in America, the labour market is stagnant. The Stockton Heath Parochial Committee at its meeting on Thursday again displayed its originality by discussing a motion by Mr. H. J. Westbrook regarding an extension of the payment of members of Parliament. ' Mr. Westbrook thinks that if payment of , members of Parliament become law the 'legislature should similarly provide out of public funds for payment of members of County Councils, Town Councils, District and Parish Councils, Boards of Guardians, Pension Committees, Education Com- mittees, School Managers, Jurymen and members of all other bodies performing public services. He argued for the resolu- tion with considerable force, though there wxs always a doubt as to whether he was serious or indulging in an elaborate joke. Mr. G.J. Brown was the principal opponent of the resolution ort the ground that it intro- duced politics into the business of the Committee, and he also refused to take Mr. Westbrook seriously. In the end, the motion was defeated by 4 votes to 3; and Mr. Lloyd-George is saved for the nonce from having to consider the monetary value to the state of the Smiths, Browns, Jones's and Wes tbrooks of parochial life.

Warrington Wesleyan Cycling Club. On Saturday a violent east wind made the members of the above club alter their whole p'an for the after- noon. Aseembling at Padget° for a ride to Poricroft thr tugh tzebury, they turned after a mile of hard work to Woolston and over the Cantilever to Warbur- ton Church. While waiting, a puncture was mended, and here a hint may be worth something. When you have found the hole take care to squeeze some solution through, whilst a friend stretches the tube. The rubber inside will make the rutside patch doubly safe," and generally prevents it from blowing off again come other day. Fr m Warburton the wind followed on, helping us •II the way to Knutsf.trd, where tea was obtained. Returning through Tabley and Pickmere to Aston Park we there turned on to the Antrobus road and reached Warrington after 33 miles easy run. Number present 20. Three Small Robbers from Latchford.

At the Warrirgton Occasional Court cn Wednesday before Messrs. F. A. Frost and Roger Parr, three boys aged between ten and twelve years, on. : William Bay, Thomas Burns and Wdliam Hewitt all of Wash-lane. Latchford, were summoned for stealing nine tennis balk from the pavilion of the Latchford Lawn Tennis Club on Sunday, May 7th.—The Acting Sergeant of Police from Stockton Beath spoke to making inquiries on receiving a complaint from Mr. , Walker, the secretary of the Club. Ife found that these three boys were jointly concerned in opening the pavilion window with the aid of a stick and a nail on the end. They got in and tAA the balls, some of 'which bad been sold.—The Bench severely cautioned the youngsters and placed them under the Probation Officer for six months, the parents being bound over in sureties of .ClO each for their good behaviour. The decease! gentleman was returning from a I business engrgement in Austria and succumbed rather suddenly to acute heart disease. Mr. Studer was born 52 years ago at Solothurn. Switzerland and after atudying chemistry at the high school there until he was 22 years of age he came to Warrington,

and took up a pos'Von as chemist with Mews. Jossph Crosfield and Sots, s tapmakert. CORONATION MUSICAL FESTIVAL.

To-day's Contests

Great interest is being manifested in Warringtm's Coronation Musical Festival, which will he held in the Parr Hall today tS.d-xrday) The entries have reached record numbers, the arrangements are perfect, and all that is needel to ensure the t•uccess of the enterprise 5 the support of the music loving public, The afternoon ses-ion at 2.30 will be presided over by his Worship the Mayor (Councillor Dr. Jos ph), and the chief event will be the male voice choir contest, for which there are eight entries, including the two local male choirs. 'the children',, and ladies' choir aapetition. and several solo contests will also be decided. At the evening sec-ion, which opens at 6.30, Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. will preside. The great event of the festival—the chief choral competitions for mixed choirs- promises to be one of the finest ever held in Lancashire, ten choirs having signified their intention of competing for twelve month', possession of the handsome challenge shield presented lry Mrs. A. H. Crosfield. , The pieces selected a e " Let the celestial concerts all unite" (Handel) and " Who is Sylvia " (German), Mrs. A. H. Crosfield is expected to be present to hand the shield to the winners. Another great attraction will be the chapel choir contest for the cup presented by the late Mr. Jame. Dodds, the entrants being Bold- Street Wesleyan last year's winners), Beweey-road Wesleyan, Stockton Heath Wesleyan sad Brick-street Mission.

lbs., will be violin solos at each session by Mr. Bradford Batton.

The all-important task of adjudication will be in the capable hands of Dr. W. G. McNaught, of London, and Mr. F. H Crossley, of Liverpool. Dr. SlcNaught, who ban been called " the prince of adjndicat rs," in one of the best known figuren in the musical woild , and was a colleague of the lwe Sir John Stliner in I the important work of munical innpection in the schools and training college, of the kingdom. He , has taken a glad iutereat in the competitive move- I ment from it. inception and his services are in great ' demand, for he is everywhere recognized as a thoroughly expert and honest critic. Mr. P. H. Crossley is also well known in musical circles, having foe twenty years been the conductor of the Warrington Musical Society which owes a great deal to 64 skilful and painstaking guidance. From 1880 to Mil' be was choirmaster at the Cathedral of Pietermaritzburg, Natal, organist of the Congrega- tional Church there, and conductor of the Philarmonic S ,ciety, afterward* completing his musical studies in Berlin.

EMPIRE DAY. THE CELEBRATIONS IN WARRINGTON SCHOOLS.

Poring the day the King received a la-r-genumber of loyal messages, one being rent by wireless by the officers and men of the Australian contingent ea route for England. His Majesty dispatched& suitable reply, At Bristul 2,500 school children paraded in costumes of red, white and blue, and were arranged in the form of a huge Union Jack. WHO IS HE?

He probably lives in your neighbourhood - in the same street, perhaps, or the nurse house. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

I)e,i,te the beautiful summer weather experienced this week the Palace and Hippodrome has been well filled each eveningdor Warrington audiences invariably appreciate a good progremme,as this one undoubtedly is. Each item bears the stamp of originality, and this in itst If is sufficient to amuse the stsreotyped English rulnd. A contribution which appeals to all sections of the audience is that if Charburn's Young Stars who while indulging in a picnic up the river impart some delightful spirit into their music and dancing. The songs, which are tuneful and cleverly conceived, met with adequate treatment on the part of the youngsters. The Englishmen's love of humorous incidents and exerting moments becomes satiated during the per- formance of Dunn and Rearm in their wonderful • acrobatic exhibition. No small amount of novelty is introduced by John S. Leick and Mabel Keith the former giving an excellent display on the cornet and the latter conducting the orchestra in a style worthy of Smra himself, diners on the cornet also betray a sound knowledge of the instrument and no further proof is required to convince the hearers of their musical capacity. It may be mentioned that Mr. Leick has performed as a soloist before his late Majesty King Edward VII. As an Indian dub swinger and juggler the likes of Leolin have not been here for a long time. Die performance is eminently pleasing, awl evokes the cordial admiration of the audience. Hampton and Barman give some delightful duets, and 'their symmetrical dancing is superb. The comedian, whistler and dancer finds adequate expression in Tich , Atherton, who while amusing others shares some of the joy himself. The pictures are particularly good, and so are the selections try the orchestra, under the leadership of Mr. E. A. Widdop.

RELIC/014

REV. J. C. TRAMPLEISURE VICAR OF WHITLEY.

Whitley Church stands in rho heart of typical Cheshire rural scenery and its many interesting features well relay a visit, It has a small but quaintly effective interior and there are two chancel.. 'l'he old carved oak corbels are supsrior examples of Oda class of work and nearly all the windows are of stained glass. A recent embellishment of the church In a handsome oak font cover. The organ (specially designed by the Vicar), is a splendid instrument, having two manuals and pedals and 23 stops. It was built by Messrs. Rushworth and Su. in 1907 at a cod of £l3O.

The —present Vicar of Whitley. the Rev. J. seph Clare Trampleasure, is doing a very active and useful work in the p irish. Born lat Liverpool in 1861, he mils educated private ly and at rrinity College. Dublin, and St. Aidan's Theological College. Ile has always been much devoted to mitaic, and before taking Holy Orders ass organist at St. Ambrose's Church, Everton, and Si. Luke's, Tranmere. He was ordained deacon in 1884, and priest in 1885 by Dr. Stubbs, Bioltop of Chester. Mr. Trampleaoure's first curacy was at St. Anne's, Birkenhead, and from 1885 to 1901 he was assistant curate at Dodleston (with charge of Higher Kinnerton). In 1800 Mr. Trampleasure acted as private secretary to the Principal ad inirriot of St. Aidan's Collage. Ile was appointed vicar of Whit'ey in 1902 by the patron, Sir P. W. Chetwode, Bart., and under his leadership the church and its organizations are making good progress. Mr. Trampleasure is very fond of athletics, and acted as cap•ain of the Boughton Hall Cricket Club in 1897-8-9. He took an active part in institu. ing the motor 'bus service, which io ouch a great convenience to dwellers in the isolated Whitley district, and was uppointecl hon. managing director of the Stretton and Whitley Motor 'Bus Co. , in 1910.

Holy Trinity Church. The annual sermons in aid of the Sunday School were preached at this Church on Sunday, in the morning by the Rev. Canon Stevenson, and in the evening by the Rev. F. W. E. Chadwick. A special scholars' service was held in the afternoon, when on address was given by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton. At the evening service the anthem " Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ " was ably rendered by the choir, Mr. Randall presiding at the organ. The collections were about the same as last year.

Illness of the Bishop of Liverpool Three Months Rest Ordered.

At the annual meeting of the Liverpool Diocesan Sunday School Institute, on Monday, Archdeacon Madden announced that the doctors bad insisted upon the Bishop (Dr. Chavasse) taking three months' rest. They had all recognised for 801319 time, said the arch- deacon, that the pressure of work on the Bishop was very likely to result in physical breakdown. His spirit, his enthusiasm, his zeal for God and His Church was stronger, the speaker thought, than his body. The Bishop could not leave until July, end Archdeacon Madden urged that Dr. Chavasse should be relieved, as , far as possible, of any extra services he had to per- form. The archdeacon did not think the Mehl(' ahould go on until July, and had so informed Ida lordship. —•— - -

„.... Mr. It. FARR Ail, the secretary, in presenting the report said that some of there would feel that the success of their work had been small but others would feel somewhat more aatisfled. The result of that work was not, however, to be measured by the attendance at their meetings but they needed to renew their efforts with a willingness to consecrate themselves to the work and to God and then they would and happineas in thought and work and the blessings of God would be sure to raward their efforts. It had ever been their desire to preach the old Gospel of Jesus Christ in Weir meet- ings, the Gospel that bettered the body, the mind and the spirit, and which was the one thing needful that could not be taken from them. It tanght them to love, , moved them to sympathy and affection, and impelled them to bear and forbear with one anther, while it tended to destroy the haseness in their nature and helped them to love their neighbours as themeelves. It also taught them how to make sacrifice. for the good of men and the Glory of God. Reference was made to the fact that during the year a deficit of £lO hod trees cleared off by special efforts. The Secretary, continu- ins, asked if this experience could not he repeated, and by their efforts and by God's help enable them to bring a larger number of members into the Brother. hood which would make their work easier end he of great service financially. This Increase of membership, however, was a mall pin compared with the building up of characters and the love of Jesus Christ. The making of good lives and good Christian fathers and brothers was of far greater importance than financial success, but given the first the other would follow. LAUNCH OF THE NAVAL AIRSHIP.

At Barrow-in-Furness, on Monday, was launched the fir.it B itish Naval Airship. She has been two years in building, and has been called the " Mystery Ship" owing to the strict secrecy of her construction. She is 512 feet in length, and 48 feet in height, of the rigid type, and contains 17 gasbags. These arc inflate] with hydrogen, and hold over 700,000 feet of gas, capable of lifting 21 tons. She c trries two 200 horse power engines, and is expected to attain a speed of from 45 to 50 miles an hour. A crew of nine men will be carried in the gondolas, fore and aft, which are connected by a covered-in passage-way. Our picture shows her moored in the middle of the dock.

MILITARY SUNDAY AT YORK. The annual special service for the military held M York Minster is always a popular --• function, and on Sunday last thousands of visitors from the West Riding and surrounding districts flocked to the City to witness the. spectacle. Long before the time fined for the arrival of the troops the streets were lined with sight-seers. The service was of an impressive character, and opened by the massed bands playing Tschaikowsky's "1812 Overture." At the conclusion of the service the troops formed tip and marched past the General Officer Commander in Chief. Altogether the troops numbered about 2000, Our oicture shows the Green Howards leaving the Minster.

ELYARRINGTON CHAPTERS.

THE FIRST RAILWAY TRAGEDY.

In a reference to the keroplane disaster by which Mr. Berteaux, Foreign Minister of France, was swept to his death, the "Daily News" recalls the tragedy that marked the opening of the first railway when several statesmen had gathered to witness Stephenson's triumph. For anything lite • ptrallel to yesterday's accident we have ii go back eighty years to the death of Iluskisson, cut down oaring what was Tactically the first pult'ic railsay journey. Since then not one ttateeruan has fallen a victim to the imtruntenta of locomotion until yesutrday. The

tempter..., like the train, he. been baptised with the bloo I of eminent men.

It was quite close to Warrington that Huskisson met his sad end and, by a coincid- ence, we were the other day copying the inscription written upon the tablet which marks the spot._

THIS TABLET

A TRIBUTE OR IRRAONIL RESPECT AND AFEE,TIOR

The dignified tablet thus inscribed, stands by the side of the North Western Railway Company at Parkside, a few paces from the road that leads beyond Winwick to Lowton. Formerly covered by a shed, it is now pro- tected from the weather by a particularly ugly arch of brickwork and by this the visitor will be able to discover it.

To tell the story in full and to explain the presence of the distinguished statesmen at the opening of the railway, would be to tell almost the whole tale of George Stephenson's great undertaking. Enough for our present purpose may be given in brief compass and the rest may be perused in the pages of Smiles' "Life of Stephenson."

GEORGE STEPHEN/lOWA TRIUMPH.

The great task had come to its close. The great cuttings from Liverpool had been made. Chat Moss had been made to yield a solid bed and lines had at last been laid from Manchester to Liverpool. The first entire trip between the two centres had been per- formed on June 14th, 1830, on the occasion of a meeting of the directors held in Manchester. The train was on this trip drawn by the "Arrow," one of the best and latest of Stephenson's locomotives. 'fhe engineer him- self drove the engine and Captain Scoresby, the circumpolar navigator, stood beside him on the foot plate,and minuted the speed of the train. A great concourse of people assembled at both ends, as well as along the line, to witness the novel spectacle of a train of carriages dragged by an engine at speed of 17 miles an hour. On the return jurney to Liverpool in the evening the "Arrow" ciossed Chat Moss at a speed of nearly 27 miles an hour, reaching its destination in about an hour and a half.

Three months work, however, remained to be done before the line was ready for passenger traffic and Stephenson spent busy days in hard work and experirnent.Especially harrassing and anxious were the experi- ments made with a view to carrying the public at so rapid a rate and by a means of which no one had had experience. Every week, for nearly three months, trial trips were made to Newton and back two or three trains generally following each other and carrying two or three hundred persons. During some experiments of this period it is interesting to know that Mr. Henry Booth,who was Stephenson's assistant,hit on the idea of the coupling screw for the secure and close joining of the carriages, a device which has been used ever since. THE OPENING OF THE FIRST RAILWAY.

The line was at length completed and the opening was announced for September 15th, 1830. Very properly the event was regarded as a national event and the opening was celebrated accordingly. The Duke of Wellington, then Prime Minister, Sir Robert Peel, and the Right Hon. William Huskisson were among those who travelled north by stage coach to have the experience of being drawn in the railway train from Liverpool to Manchester. Eight locomotive engines, constructed at the Stephenson works, had been placed upon the line, all of them tested by a week or two of trial. The several trains accommodated in all about 600 persons and vast crowds assembled all along the route to applaud them as they passed. The procession passed between a living line of spectators as it ran through the ravine of Olive Mount ; up the Sutton incline; over the Sankey Viaduct—carriages filling the narrow lanes, the barges crowding the river, the people below gazing with wonder and admiration at the trains which sped along the line, far above their heads at the rate of some 24 miles an hour. THE TRAGEDY AT PARICSIDE.

At Parkside, the engines stopped to take in water, and it was here that the accident occurred which threw gloom over all, and, in the eloquent words of the memorial : " Changed a moment of the noblest exalta- tion into one of desolation and mourning." The train which contained the Duke of Wellington and the other statesmen was drawn by the engine "Northumbrian " and had been placed on one line in order that the whole of the trains might pars in review before them upon the other. Mr. Huskisson had stepped down from the carriage to examine the lipes, and did not notice that the " Rocket " was coming rapidly towards them to begin the " review." There was a general cry of danger and the call "Get in, get in." But Huskisson had been ill, and flurried and confused he endeavoured to get round the open door of the carriage which projected over the opposite rail. Before he could do so he was struck down by the "Rocket." The Duke of Wellington had seiaed big hand and tried to draw him from danger, but his help ppt avail. The engine threw him across the line, and falling with his leg across the metal, the limb was instantly crushed, "I have met my death" he moaned as they lifted him from the ground, a word that was unhappily true for ha died the same evening. There was no medical help at Parkside and Stephenson volunteered to carry him on the 'engine "Northumbrian "to Eccles. He did so,and Huskisson died at the parsonage there after a few hours of suffering,

This forced journey showed e capacity of the locomotive, for Stephen o drove over the 15 miles in 25 minutes, a record of speed which broke upon the world with the effect of a new and marvellous phenomenon. The accident put gloom over the great day of triumph. Sir Robert Peel and Wellington desired that the train should return to Liver- pool, but it was pointed out that the vast crowds at Manchester would be disappointed,' that report would exaggerate the mischief and that panic might seriously interfere with the future and repute of the railway. The party did so on the understanding that all further festivity after that should be abandoned. Wild scenes were witnessed at Man- chester. The crowds, becoming excited at the news which had reached them, had broken bounds and would not be restrained even by the military. They crowded around the train holding on by the door handles, but no fatal accident occurred.

The Duke of Wellington, who was unpopular, was singled out for showers of stones, and placards about " Peterloo" were exhibited. The Duke did not leave the carriage but shook hands with some of the crowd, and the train soon began the return journey, which, after many interruptions was completed at a late hour. One incident brightens the gloom of the memory of Huskisson's death. He and the Duke had been estranged and a complete coolness had come between them, but in the moment of danger the Duke leaned forward to offer his hand. Huskisson grasped it with warmth and evident recognition of a kindness that lay behind the action. Though but a few seconds intervened, the two states- men smiled kindness to each other and were reconciled.

The next morning 140 passengers booked to travel by train and did the jonrney in two hours. And so began the great see-saw between the two cities which has continued ever since. AM.

CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB.

THE SMALLEST LOAF.

Long years ago, a great famine brought suffering to the people of Germany. In the land there was a rich man who loved children. He sent for 20, and said to thorn : "There is in this basket a loaf of bread for each of you. Take it, and come back again every day until the famine is ended, and each day I will give you a loaf." Now the children were all of them hungry, so hungry that they could not even wait to thank their kind friend. They grasped the basket, and fought to get the largest loaf. So they quarrelled and snatched at the bread, and soon every one ran away with his loaf, except little Gretchen, who stood alone at a distance smiling at the gentleman and thank- ing him from her heart. She took up the last loaf, which war the smallest of them all, and went away. The children came again next day. They did not behave any better than before. Gretchen, as before, did not quarrrel and push, but stood aside, and to-day she received a tiny loaf, indeed one that was hardly half as large as the others. But when she reached home with it, and her mother began to cut it, six shining coins of silver rolled out. "0, Gretchen, this must be a mistake," the mother cried. "This money does not belong to us; run as quickly as you can, and take it back to the kind gentleman." So Gretchen took it back to her friend, but when she gave her mother's message the gentleman said : "No, it wasn't a mistake. I had the silver baked into the smallest loaf in order to reward you. Remember that one who is content to have a small loaf instead of quarrelling for a large one will find bless- ings better than money baked in bread." THIS WEEK'S STORY.

Tommy: "Pa, what is an equinox?" Pa: Why, er—it is—ahem! Tommy, , don't you know anything about mythology at all? An equinox was a fabled animal, half horse, half cow. Its name is derived from the words 'equine,' which means horse and 'ox,' which of course means a cow. It does seem as if those public schools don't teach children anything nowadays!"

FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, N P

THE INSURANCE BILL AND SOME

OBJECTORS.

The discussion which is going on about the Insurance Bill is showing what an extremely difficult and complicated question it is. As its provisions become better known, and as the effect of them upon interests touched by the proposals is more fully appreciated, it is not unlikely that a very considerable opposi- tion to the Bill may be accumulated. It is impossible to touch such a big question as this without interfering with existing interests. No reform over was or ever will be carried out without doing an injury to some innocent persona. Progress always claims its innocent victims. The taxi-cab, for instance, is ruining men whose capital was invested in horse drawn vehicles, and taking away the livelihood of the drivers of horses, many of whom are too old to learn to work the new motor cab. It would pass the wit of man to devise some plan by which compensation could be given to every interest which is injuriously affected by change„

The Chancellor of the Exchequer has done all that was possible to avoid coming up against the most powerful interests which would be affected by a national system of insurance, namely the great insurance corn- ' panics and the friendly societies. He has bought off the opposition of the first named by avoiding all interference with insurance for death payments, and the friendly societies have been quietened by having been given the most important part to play in the management of the national sickness insur- ance scheme. In the unemployment part of the scheme he has done as much as was humanly possible to reconcile the trade unions to the plan, by making them the agencies through which the benefits may be distributed. But in spite of all he has done to lessen opposition it has not all been removed, and I am afraid it is growing every day:

There are certain friendly societies which are excluded from the privileges of becoming lagencies for the distribution of the State fund. They are not satisfied, and they are making their disaffection known. It would have been impossible to have permitted all the existing friendly societies to come within the scheme and the grievance of the small societies is removed to a great extent by a provision which enables them to join together so as to qualify as an approved society. THE CASE OF THE DOCTORS. •

As one listened to the Chancellor of the Exchequer eloquently sympathising with the woes of the club doctor under the present conditions, and promising that he should be better treated as the State Insurance medical officer, it seemed as if the support of the doctors would have been secured by this promise of higher pay But the Medical Associations are organising against the Bill in so far as its provisions affect them, and are putting forward counter proposals, some of which seem reasonable. We have to take human nature into account in framkg pro- posals which deal with human beings. There is prejudice against the club doctor, and many people will prefer to pay for a doctor of their own choosing rather than have the doctor who is sent to them by the club or the State.

The Insurance Scheme will never work unless the medical service is so organised that under it the patients will have a choice of doctors and given the opportunity,as far as possible, of having the medical man they have come to regard as the " family doctor." It should not be impossible to do this. Instead of having doctors attached to societies and requiring all the members of that society to have the doctor attached to that society, the medical men might be attached to the system, and the members might be allowed to select any one of the doctors approved by the State. It is certain that the success of the scheme in operation will depend to a very great extent on the way in which the medical service is organised.

SOME TRADE UNION FEARS. There are some trade unions who are fearing that the Scheme may indirectly injure them. It has been very difficult to organise the low paid workman because he really cannot afford to pay the 2d. or 3d. a week which membership of a trade union involves. Many workmen of the general labourer class on wages of much less than a pound a week are now struggling to pay into their trade union. But if they have to pay 4d. a week to the State Sickness Insur- ance Fund and 21d. a week to the Unemploy- ment Insurance, they will not be able to do it without giving up something which they are now paying, and there is a real danger that the trade union may be the sufferer. This possibility might be got over, perhaps, by an extension of an idea already relieving the low paid labourer of the compulsory con- tribution altogether or reducing it to a very small sum. Under 15s. a week there is a reduced premium to be paid under the Bill as it stands, but if the wage figure were fixed at say £1 for the reduced premium the reasonable fear which the leaders of the Labourers Union now have as to the serious effect which the compulsory puyment will have on trade unions may be renderd groundless. THE CONDITIONS OF PAY.

The Unemployment part of the Bill is in the hands of the Board of Trade. A very elaborate actuarial statement has beenl issued on which the financial benefits under I this part of the Bill are based. The cost of the scheme as at present framed will prob- ably be proportionately higher than if other trades had been included. One reason why a number of the big industries like the rail- ways and the miners are left out of the com- pulsory unemployment insurance is probably because there would have been an objection I on the part of these industries, which have relatively little unemployment, to contribute towarde the support of other trades where the percentage is very high. But all trades are to do this to some extent under the I present limited extent of the scheme. The Memorandum which has been issued explaining the scope of the Unemployment part of the Bill has made a few points clear which were left obscure in the brief references which the Chancellor made to this part. About two and a ball' million men will come within the scope of this compulsory unem- ployment insurance at the outset. The definition of the general terms "building," I"construction of works," "shipbuilding," ..

etc., is very wide,and will'includera-gOodcie-a.l . .

more of the subiddiary trades thtui was at one time thought. Under the beading of "building." all workmen who are employed in the construction, repair, decoration, or demolition of a building are to be included. This will bring masons, bricklayers,painters

SOME ONEROUS REGULATIONS.

The conditions of receiving the unemploy- ment benefit are somewhat stringent. A person must have been employed at one of the trades within the Act for not lees than 26 weeks. He is not entitled to benefit when he is unemployed as the result of a strike or lock-out. There is a rather dangerous con- dition that a workman who has been dis- charged from his employment on account of his misconduct shall not be entitled to claim out of work pay until six weeks after his I dismissal. This is a power which will have to be used with much care, or cases of hard- ship may arise where an employer dismisses a man because he has a grudge against him and makes misconduct the reason. The workman is protected by having given to I him the right to submit his cage to a Court of Referees on which workmen and employers are equally represented. A workman who has been in prison is not to claim out of work pay until he has been out for six weeks, and any workman who voluntarily leaves his work without just cause is not to be entitled to benefit until the expiration of six weeks. This latter condition will probably lead to more friction than any other regulation. On the other hand there is a regulation that a man shall not be deprived of his right to out of work pay because he has refused a job at a lower rate ot wages than he is usually paid or a rate which is lower than the current rate. This is a regulation which is very necessary if this scheme is not to be a help to the under- cutting employer. If a workman cannot get a job, and it appears to the authorities that this is due to his incompetence, the insurance officer may arrange to have this workman 'given a special course of industrial training and may pay for this out of the general funds. This is a very valuable provision, and one which may be extended so as to become a very useful adjunct indeed to the general scheme.

OUR SERIAL STORY.

[ALL RI,: FITS It. SERVED.)

A YYSTERY OF IdAYFAIII.

JOHN STRANGE WINTER.

CHAPTER XXVII. NIOGINS ON TIM JOB.

"Down Cretuir, down, good dog," said Lawson, patting Simeon'n bloodhound on the head. The huge dog gambolled round and round, his long tail wagging fast and furi- ously, his jaws apart. He was overjoyed at meeting his master's friend on the road be- tween the two houses. The hound's deep bay broke the thread of Lawson's disjointed thoughts. He felt that he most talk to some- one, and he gladly made a confidant of the faithful and intelligent dog. -

Lawson could not immediately grasp the sig- nificance of Miriam's disclosure. Would he ever accomplish the task which he had set himself? 'there was a vast conspiracy afoot, the meaning of which was beyond his under- standing. It wan, at present, unfathomable. He compared himself to a vessel in the midst of contrary winds, buffeted here and there, till his bark was finally ahipwrecked on the jagged rocks of Hopeless Despair. When would dawn the day of Victory? Alas, the day and the hour were apparently still in the far distant future. "Tine son of a slave of the harem. God of Heaven, it in maddening." He still retained in his hand the miniature he had shown to Miriam Trafford. Unclaspieg his fingers, he gazed in the sunlight at the perfect features, depicted by an artist of rare skill and deli- cacy of touch. "And I flattered nivitelf this angelic face was that of my mother,' he mur- mured. .

There was something sadder than tears in the deep pathos of his melancholy voice and the hound whined in sympathy with him. " Does the blood of a polygamist and • odoleaque flow in my veins," he exclaimed, striking his brow with Isis clenched fist. " I'll not believe they are the author's of my existence, and as I live by bread, I'll prove the truth or fabwhood of that woman's venomous words." he poignancy of his grief was terrible, for it seemed to him that life had become a ahem., and a torture. Should he bring his disgrace to an end and diet The blessing of an honoured life was denied him, then why live, loathing himself? Yes, he would die—and yet what of Aide. Could he leave her all alone to suffer hideously, while he rushed into the Unknown, seeking ever- lasting peace in a suicide's grave? No, he would not be guilty of self-murder. He mar- velled in him despair why Miriam had not sent for the police, and given bins into cus- tody for theft. There he saw salvation, for he realised she wax deceiving him.

He -looked down with a tart, and noticed for the first time that old Ctesar was licking shin hands. Tho touch of the dog's tongue brought other and bettor thought-1 to his mind. As he stroked the animar• bend, the expression of his face grew more resigned. The die was cast. lie would, could conquer his ill-starred destiny, and live. Lawson flung himself down on the grass by the side of the river, SIId moodily snatched the current flowing toward the weir. The fatigue of travelling, and the combined ex- citement of the morning, produced an over- whelming desire for rest and he dropped into sensi•unconseiousness from which he was rudely awakened by the growling of the hound, still lying at his feet. Michael lifted • his head crossly, for he had heel enjoying visions of happiness during his drowsy slop, • freedom from care, his troubles were fading Into nothingness. Mr. Croslield and National Service.

I r,—Your unknown corrraportient " Veritas," does not appear to relish Mr. Crosfield's criticism of the Notion/el Service League and Iris reference to Conscription and Tariff Reform as deal mill-stones round the necks if the workers. Mr. Crostield was perfectly right in twitting those menders of the League who voted for Protection with being false to their principles, and " Veritas "only makes the case stronger by pointing cut how many leading National Service Leaguers in IVartingten are Free Traders. Did they vote for Free Trade or for National Serv;ce? 1 suppose it was merely a question of which ',Hoy was dearest to their hearts. One et. two Free Trade National Service men, we do know, voted for Mr. Harold Smith.

Ve,ioe' calmly accwes Mr. Crosfield of trying to " mislead the public "—a crime the ex-Member's record es a speaker and politician completely acquits him of. One cannot a.y as much for "Veritas." who coma ences his letter by a deliberate distortion of tl.e facts when he says " Mr. Crosfield . . . ttied to throw dust in the

eyes of his andience by suggesting that the supporters of National Service are ipso Into supporters of taxing the peoples foal." Mr. Crosfirld never I *um sled anything so fatuous,and your corresp,ndent knows this perfectly W. 11, lut he seems too anxio to embroil Mr. Cresfield with a section of his con- stituer ts to care nlie,her or no his methods are clean and above-hoard. . . .

Sir, —" Veritas " is entirely mistaken in suggesting that I tried to throw du.t in anyone's eyes, when I referred in the course of my speech at Bridge Foot on June 9th, to the action taken at the last Election by some membere of the National Service League. And he is not entitled to assume from the rep,: of my speech, that I brought any charge against members of the Naticnal Service League en bloc. I see, however, that there is solos excuse for the error into which he has fallen. You gave an excellent report of my remarks, but as I spoke at considerable length, some abbreviation was necessary. Had " Veritas " heard the speech, it would bee been c!elr Icr him that my criticism was directed to those members of the National Service League, and to those alone, who while Liberal or Parlical by convicti ,n, voted Tory at the last Election. 'I hey did no with the expressed object of helping on the cause of compulsory military service. In other words their object is to get the country to consent to swallow a mild do.e of conscrip- tion.

Well now, whether the respont.ible leader of the Conservative party in the House of Cotnmons will adopt conscription as a phmk in his patty's political platform, remains to be seen in the future. Sa far Mr. Balfour has taken precious good care not to do so. He probably foresees that if once it becomes committed to tlds policy, there is a worse fate in store for his party than even that severe slump in Toryism it net in with the ill-omened effort to re-introduce Protect'. n. . . .

I can only call to mind one man who has come out into the open to fight an election on this specific issee. That man was Captain Kincaid Smith. He fought as the chosen protagoeist of the National Service League, and met with overwhelming deft at at tho polls. The fact is the great majority of people recognise that no case has been made out for the introduction of compulsory military service into this country, and foiling a very strong case in support of the proposal they are reeolntely opposed on many grounds to the system. But unless a powerful inajority declares in favour of it, there is really not the slightest chance of this system being adopted in England. That in a patent fact admitted by practical men on all sides, and there- fore this question of compulsory military service was in no real sense a aital practical issue of present day ' politics at the last election Everyone knows that the great Censtitutional and fiscal issues held the field. And the defence of Free Trade, giving as Free Trade does give our people in the United Kingdom, their bent opportunities for securing permanent and profitable employment ; giving them purity in their government ; and guaranteeing to all people what to every one of modest means is a matter of the first importance, namely, moberate and reasonable prices for food and clothing—rarely, that defence of Free Trade would

, seem to he a matter of paramount importance to every one who tete a high value on the physical and motel well-being of the people. Now the members of the National Service League are constantly—and very properly, reminding Us of the supreme importance to the Nation of physical fitness. And that is ‘vlty I criticised those members of the League who, although convinced Liberals end Free Traders, yet voted for Protection last I,ecember. I asked them in my speech with " what shred of consistency " they could give those votes for Pro- tection, and I ask them do saline question now. The more one considers boo Pi otection would undermine the vitality of our people and their capacity, whether in Commerce or in war, the more One realises that the vot., that I have just alluded ti were in Lct the very arme of inconsistency; . . . .

—And- if it be urged in defence of them that it W3B worth while to run the link of the over-throw of Free Trade to-day, for the take of some problematical advantage to the cause of tie League in the future, 1 think this is a pretty good illustration of want of sense of proport on into the bargain. It also strikes me that considering what has been at stake in our recent history and evolution as a nation, such votes given at such a time were a mighty queer way of prowl! g loyalty and fidelity to Liberal principles.—Yonra Ac., A IL CROSFIELD.

Mr. New acd His Critic.

Sir,—May I crave your indulgence to endeavour to set Mr James Hammon's mind at refl. Ile quotes me as saying that a certain firm in Battersea, had. awirg to the introduction of new machinery, been able to produce a certain class of goods at a much quicker rate than formerly ; alto one man could now do the work of sin, e nsequently the displaced men were distnitoed, the gentleman responsible being a Tory M.P., and a Tariff Reformer. He went further and said I emphasised the fact, and in Ids opinion I was playing the game rather low. If stating facts means playing the game low it seems to me most garnen in politics are played rather high, but my friend is not accurate in his facto, neither are his interpretations correct. What I said was that Tariff Reformers were continually throwing dust in the eyes of the workers by pomising higher wages and mire employment—a very tempting bait. I quoted the case of a large !laver-ea firm who, during the last 11 years, had doubled the number of their employees and extended their premises on all sides under Free Trade, yet boy labour had largely nap- planted men. Wages had gradually declined in ninny inetartces owing to the townsmen having, to com- pete n ith the agriculural labourer lobo was prevented from developing his natural resources, not through the foreigner, hut by the land owner. I said the head of the firm went about preaching that Tariff Reform meant higher wagers and more munk, knowing full well it was not true. But I hardly eee me socialist friend's print. I always endeavour to deal with facts as I find them and shall always welcome criticism, hut let it he honest. My advice to Mr. Hampson is when in doubt tackle the speaker on the spot. I arn glad he admits, thmgh grudgingly, Free Trade is better than Protection, and trust lie will help as when the next nisi of strength may come. I ant afraid he has allowed Iris judgment to be somewhat narrow, but I will give him credit for trying to be sincere.—Yours very truly,

tl2, Norris-street, Warrington, 21th 11tty, 1911.

HARRY NEW.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE Rogues of the Turf. '

Bri-mnean of action, a great variety of incident, and a pleasant blending rf the exciting and the comic make Max Goldberg's sporting comedy drama "Rogues , of the Turf," which is presented at the Royal Court Theatre this week, an attraction nf much above the average merit. Although it is concerned chiefly with the doings of the racing fraternity it has a strong general Interest and there is a welcome lack of the boring time-killing deviens which in too many cams are allowed Fa break the continuity of dramatic stories. The three rogues, headed by Captain Dudley Clifton, ate of the unscrupulous type dear to the writer of melodrama and stick at nothing to further emir own sordid interests In their attempt to " nobble " the favourite for the Manchester cup they are, however, neatly foiled and Forest King wins the trophy after a thrilling and highly realistic racecourse scene. Mr. John F. Preston's company does full justice to the play, and the performance goes with a awing which compels attention aid frequently arouses keen excite- ment. Mr. Preston takaa the part of Joe Flaxman, a trainer, in homely, unaffected yet forceful style and proves a great favourite with the audience. Miss Mahelle F. Barlow, as Mario., an imposter with several aliases, &t ea a spirited and convinring portrayal and Mr. John Richter, as Captain Dndlay Clifton, makes a thorough-going villain. Ile has two equally shady accomplices in Moses Solomon. (Mr. W. H. Barlow) and Jena Stronger!. (Mr. Tom Phillips) who have much to do with the working out of the plot. Mr. Arthur Leighton is a manly, dashing Steve Barton, the Jockey, Mr. Don F. Roe does eeil as Sammy Flaxman, the irrepressible apprentice ; Mira Marjorie Palgra•e is a winst me Nellie Flaxman, and Miss nevi., Gale creates merr - meet as Phyllis Wurzel Mr. Edwin Morton presents ' a capital study of Sir Gerald Ileatheote, a sporting baronet. Nit the lea-t important members of the company are the three thoroughbred racehorses which add much to the realism of the play. The mounting is in the best of taste and the orchestra, tinder Mr. Boothroyd's conductorship, earns applause Iny well- varied selections. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRA \IME.
